Language:
Effects
We’ve been holding this off for too long now, so here it comes: with_fx!
That’s a powerful tool for adding different effects to your composition. Let’s start with :reverb. Pretty much everything sounds nice with a reverb. Start with surrounding your code with a with_fx :reverb do and end blocks:
live_loop :effects do
with_fx :reverb do
play 50
sleep 0.5
sample :elec_plip
sleep 0.5
play 62
end
end
Sounds bigger, doesn’t it? Just like synths and samples, effects also have options. mix:is an option that sets how much the effect and the original sound is heard. mix: 0 plays only the original sound, mix: 1 plays only the FX. Reverb has a room option also. Try different room sizes with values between 0-1. If you open the ‘Fx’ tab on the help menu you can find what options are available for which FX.
live_loop :effects do
with_fx :reverb, mix: 0.5, room: 0.9 do
play 50
sleep 0.5
sample :elec_plip
sleep 0.5
play 62
end
end
There are so many FX to choose from. And you don’t even have to settle for one: you can nest them! (At some point your computer can run out of resources but don’t worry about that now.)
live_loop :effects do
with_fx :wobble do
with_fx :slicer do
with_fx :reverb do
play 50
sleep 0.5
sample :elec_plip
sleep 0.5
play 62
end
end
end
end
Now go crazy and add FX everywhere for some amazing new sounds!

Generate sounds
You have a lot covered already. In this chapter you’ll learn a bit more about how to use chord and scale, iterate through lists and use variables. The aim is to take the composition into a more generative direction, where parts of the song are generated based on parameters that you can modify on the fly.

Language:
Tick
Remember chord? The chord function gives you the notes of a certain chord:
play (chord :c, :major).choose
# plays a random note of the C major chord (:c, :e or :g)
There’s also a function called scale. Scale returns all the notes in a scale, not just the ones in a chord:
play (scale :c, :major).choose
# plays a random note of the C major scale (:c, :d, :e, :f, :g, :a or :b)
With choose you can get a random element from a list. If you want to go through the values in a more structured manner, Sonic PI has a very powerful function called tick:
live_loop :arp do
play (scale :e3, :minor_pentatonic).tick, release: 0.1
sleep 0.125
end
Here, we’re just grabbing the scale E3 minor pentatonic and ticking through each element. This is done by adding .tick to the end of the scale declaration. This tick is local to the live loop, so each live loop can have its own independent tick:
live_loop :arp do
play (scale :e3, :minor_pentatonic).tick, release: 0.1
sleep 0.125
end
live_loop :arp2 do
use_synth :dsaw
play (scale :e2, :minor_pentatonic, num_octaves: 3).tick, release: 0.25
sleep 0.25
end
Rings
You can tick through anything that is a ring (well, you can tick through lists also but it will stop when you get to the end). Ring is a special list, that starts over when you get to the end. Like in the previous example the scale started again from the beginning after reaching the last note. scale and chord both return a ring. Sometimes you’ll want to create a list and turn that into a ring by calling .ring or using the ring creator:
puts [1, 2, 3, 4].ring #=> (ring 1, 2, 3, 4)
puts (ring 1, 2, 3, 4) #=> (ring 1, 2, 3, 4)
live_loop :arp do
play [:c, :e, :d, :f].ring.tick, release: 0.1
sleep 0.125
end
Here’s a bit more complex example. Here you have a list or chords that is turned into a ring and ticked through:
live_loop :keys do
use_synth :blade
play [(chord :Ab, :major7), (chord :Db, :major7), (chord :Bb, :minor7), (chord :Eb, '7')].ring.tick, attack: 0.1, release: 1
sleep 1
end
And then top it off with a lead ‘melody’:
live_loop :keys do
use_synth :blade
play [(chord :Ab, :major7), (chord :Db, :major7), (chord :Bb, :minor7), (chord :Eb, "7")].ring.tick, attack: 0.1, release: 1
sleep 1
end
live_loop :lead do
use_synth :dsaw
play (scale :Ab, :major, num_octaves: 3).mirror.tick, attack: 0.05, release: 0.25, cutoff: rrand(80, 110), amp: 0.5
sleep 0.25
end
Start ticking, go wild!

Language:
Variables
Take a look at the example below. What’s going on?
live_loop :melody do
use_synth :blade
r = [0.25, 0.25, 0.5, 1].choose
play (chord :c, :minor).choose, attack: 0, release: r
sleep r
end
There’s a variable r that gets a certain value each time the loop is played and that value is being used as the release option for play and as the sleep length. A variable is a bit like a box where you can store things and take things out. Using a variable is as simple as variable_name = variable_value. Now the variable_value is stored in variable_name. You can get the value just by typing variable_name.
Let’s add a synth and bass to the example to try out variables. The :keys loop is simple, nothing new happening, but the :bass loop is a bit tricky:
live_loop :melody do
use_synth :blade
r = [0.25, 0.25, 0.5, 1].choose
play (chord :c, :minor).choose, attack: 0, release: r
sleep r
end
live_loop :keys do
use_synth :blade
play chord(:c, :minor)
sleep 1
end
live_loop :bass do
use_synth :fm
n = (chord :c2, :minor).tick
3.times do
play n
sleep 1
end
play n
sleep 0.5
play (chord :c2, :minor).tick
sleep 0.5
end
n = (chord :c2, :minor).tick takes a note from a C minor chord and saves it to a variable named n. .tick always moves forward to the next value after it’s called. play n plays the saved note. Then .tick is called again to get the next note from the chord. When the loop starts again, .tick continues from where it was.

Language:
Chord progressions
The previous example was playing only in C minor. Here’s an example that uses a ring of chords that all the loops use. All the loops will play around the same chord and one loop is changing the chord for all. It’s getting quite complex, but check it out for inspiration if you wish. You can use the chord progression charts for choosing nice chords if you wish.
chords = [(chord :C, :minor7), (chord :Ab, :major7), (chord :Eb, :major7), (chord :Bb, "7")].ring
c = chords[0] # take the first chord of the ring and save it to a variable
# this is going to be used in all the live_loops. It will be ticked by the :bass loop
live_loop :melody do
use_synth :blade
r = [0.25, 0.25, 0.5, 1].choose
play c.choose, attack: 0, release: r
sleep r
end
live_loop :keys do
use_synth :blade
play c
sleep 1
end
live_loop :bass do
use_synth :fm
use_octave -2
3.times do
play c[0] # play the first note of the chord
sleep 1
end
play c[2] # play the third note of the chord
sleep 0.5
play c[1] # # play the second note of the chord
sleep 0.5
c = chords.tick
end

Language:
Sample Slicing
Let’s take a step back and take a look at samples again. Watch the video below to understand how the playback of a looping sample behaves.
It shows the playback of the :loop_amen sample with the following code:
sample :loop_amen
Now let’s make a live_loop that keeps playing the beat for us. Do you remember the option beat_stretch for the sample command that alters the pitch of the sample to match the desired length in beats? We introduced it briefly in the chapter “Tweak the sounds”. Next we’re going to use it to stretch the sample loop_amen to 4 beats and keep looping it:
use_bpm 145
live_loop :drumloop do
sample :loop_amen, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 4
end
Next we’re going to take a look how you can play parts of the Amen loop in a different order. With the sample command you can set a different starting and ending point for the sample by using parameters start and finish. Both of the parameters accept values between 0 and 1 indicating the starting and finishing points of the sample playback. For example: 0 is the beginning of the sample, 0.5 is the midpoint of the sample and 1 is the ending point of the sample. Let’s try it in action with the following example:
use_bpm 145
live_loop :drumloop do
sample :loop_amen, beat_stretch: 4, start: 0, finish: 0.125
sleep 0.5
sample :loop_amen, beat_stretch: 4, start: 0, finish: 0.125
sleep 0.5
sample :loop_amen, beat_stretch: 4, start: 0.5, finish: 0.75
sleep 1
sample :loop_amen, beat_stretch: 4, start: 0.25, finish: 0.5
sleep 1
sample :loop_amen, beat_stretch: 4, start: 0.75, finish: 0.875
sleep 0.5
sample :loop_amen, beat_stretch: 4, start: 0.25, finish: 0.375
sleep 0.5
end
The video below (created with the help of Simpler in Ableton Live) visualizes what parts of the loop_amen sample are being played in each run of live_loop:
Now isn’t it fun to play different parts of the sample in a completely different order? Try experimenting with options for the command sample. For example, adding an option rate: -1 to one of the loop_amen hits makes it play backwards.
One sample can be sculpted into almost anything once you start experimenting with the sample options (such as rate, pan, amp, attack, release, start and finish). The following example uses these options with randomized values to play unique microsounds from two samples:
use_bpm 180
with_fx :reverb, mix: 0.2, room: 1 do
live_loop :grain1 do
s = rrand(0, 0.7)
t = rrand(0.03, 0.3)
e = s + t
a = rrand(0.5, 1.0)
sample :guit_e_fifths, rate: -1.5, start: s, finish: e, attack: t/6.0, sustain: t/2.0, pan: rrand(-0.5, 0.5)
sleep t
end
live_loop :grain2 do
s = rrand(0, 0.7)
t = rrand(0.03, 0.2)
e = s + t
a = rrand(0.5, 1.0)
sample :guit_em9, rate: 1.5, start: s, finish: e, attack: t/6.0, sustain: t/2.0, pan: rrand(-0.5, 0.5)
sleep t
end
end
This technique is also widely used in granular samplers and synthesizers.

Language:
External Samples
Sonic Pi comes with approximately 164 samples that you can freely use and play around with, but it also fully supports using external samples. For example, maybe you would like to record something (like your own voice or guitar) and have it in Sonic Pi to use in your song.
First, you’ll have to have some audio samples in WAV format placed in a folder on your hard drive. In the following examples, we’re using a free sample pack by Mehackit. It is called Solenoid Samples 1 and you can download it from here. It contains 14 one-shot and looping samples that were recorded from a kinetic sound installation we created in a workshop in early 2016.
Once you have downloaded the pack and extracted the files to a folder, you’ll have to check the full path to that folder. For example, if you extracted the files to a folder called ‘Samples’ on your Desktop the path is most likely following:
Windows: “C:/Users/sam/Desktop/Samples”
Raspberry Pi, Linux and Mac: “/Users/sam/Desktop/Samples”
Just remember to use your own username instead of ‘sam’. The sample pack contains following files: hit_1.wav to hit_7.wav (percussive hits) and loop_1.wav to loop_7.wav (looping beats are recommended to be played with the sampler opt beat_stretch).
Now you can play them directly with the sample command by using the right file path:
sample "/Users/sam/Desktop/Samples/loop_1.wav", amp: 1.5
If the path was set correctly for the sample command you should now hear the sound loop_1.wav. Just remember to use your own file path instead of the one shown in these examples! That is pretty straightforward way to access and play samples. However, you would probably like to write the folder path only once in the code and play the samples by only referring to their filenames. You can declare a variable for the file path and use that in conjunction with the sample command. After sample you can enter the variable name containing the file path and then the name of the sample. We’ll declare a variable for the sample folder file path called solenoids in the example below. When you run it, the sample loop_4.wav should start playing and looping:
solenoids = "/Users/sam/Desktop/Samples/"
use_bpm 110
live_loop :solenoid1 do
sample solenoids, "loop_4", beat_stretch: 4, amp: 2
sleep 4
end
Now you’re able to use external samples and samples from Sonic Pi’s own library in your productions. Try playing the example below that uses four different instances of live_loop to play external and Sonic Pi’s own samples. Please note that in the live_loop :solenoid2 we’re using a variable samplename to randomly select one of the samples from hit_1.wav to hit_7.wav.
solenoids = "/Users/sam/Desktop/Samples/"
use_bpm 110
live_loop :solenoid1 do
sample solenoids, "loop_4", beat_stretch: 4, amp: 2
sleep 4
end
live_loop :kick do
sample :bd_haus, rate: 0.8, amp: 2
sleep 1
end
live_loop :solenoid2 do
samplename = ["hit_1", "hit_2", "hit_3", "hit_4", "hit_5", "hit_6", "hit_7"].choose
time = [0.25, 0.5, 0.75].choose
sample solenoids, samplename, amp: 1.5, rate: 2, pan: rrand(-0.8, 0.8)
sleep time
end
with_fx :reverb do
live_loop :solenoid3 do
sleep 2
sample solenoids, "hit_6", rate: (ring 0.5, 0.55, 0.6, 0.65).tick, amp: 1.5
sleep 2
end
end

Language:
Exporting Your Track
When you’re happy with your track you might want to save the track as an audio file and share it with your friends online (for example, on your SoundCloud page). First, you’ll need to record your song by using the record function in Sonic Pi.
The recording process is simply following:
Click the Rec button and it should highlight
Now that record is toggled on start your song by clicking Run
Once your song is finished (or you’ve played enough of your live_loops) click Stop
Finally, click Rec again to stop the recording
Save your audio file in the dialog window (for example, with a name MySong.wav)
Once you have your audio file saved you are ready to play and share it. However, we recommend you to process your audio file a little bit to make it sound more professional and louder. For this, we are going to use Audacity which is a free, open-source software for recording and Editing sounds. You can download Audacity (for Windows, Linux and OS X) from here: http://www.audacityteam.org/download/.
Audacity: Removing Silence
Usually when you record audio with Sonic Pi there will be some extra silence in the beginning and the end of the song. If you want, you can remove this unwanted silence with few simple steps. First, open your audio file in Audacity from the menu “File / Open”. Once you have opened the file you should see a view like the one below.
Now navigate to the “Effect” menu and select “Truncate Silence”.
Use the following values in the “Truncate Silence” window:
Level: -60 dB
Duration: 0.5 seconds
Truncate to: 0.1 seconds
Next, click “Ok”.
Now you should see the silent parts of the audio removed from the waveform.
Audacity: Normalizing loudness
Next, let’s make the audio as loud as possible without compressing or doing any damage to the audio itself. If you feel like your music is more beat-driven and should overall be louder, then this next step is for you. On the other hand, if your material is more subtle (like ambient music) and should remain like that, then we don’t necessarily recommend you to take the following steps for normalizing the audio. Anyway, here’s how you do it: Go to the “Effect” menu and select “Normalize”.
Set the following value in the “Normalize” window:
Normalize maximum amplitude” to -0.1 dB
Check the two topmost checkboxes and click “Ok”.
Now you should see that the spikes in the waveform are taller which means that the resulting audio is louder as well.
Audacity: Saving an MP3 file
Now you’ll just need to save the audio file. For example, if you want to save your track as an MP3 file, open the “File” menu and select “Export Audio”.
In the Export Audio window you should see a pull-down menu somewhere around the file browser. Select “MP3 Files” from that menu. You can use one of the of standard presets in the Format Options for the MP3 file. For example, the following Format Options should always work decently:
Bit Rate Mode: Preset
Quality: Standard, 170-210 kbps
Variable Speed: Fast
Channel Mode: Joint Stereo
Next you’ll need to enter the filename to the field “Name” and click “Save”. That should do the trick! Now you have the MP3 file of your track, which you can easily send to your friends, post on SoundCloud or upload to your mobile phone.
We hope you’ll have fun creating and publishing tracks with Sonic Pi!

Keys, chords and scales
Sometimes composing a melody with Sonic Pi can be tricky, if you don’t immediately know what 62 or :c3 sounds like. To help with that, here’s a browser keyboard for trying out your ideas. If you want, you can select a scale from the drop menu to see what keys might sound nice in a certain key.
Select scale:
C Major
Range [C3-B5]
(Use left/right arrows to adjust with keyboard)
Audio Synthesizer, created by Keith William Horwood
What chords and notes to play?
Obviously you can play whatever notes and chords you like to. There’s no right or wrong. Music doesn’t have to sound harmonic and some dissonance or noise belongs to a track as much as the artist wants it to belong. However there’s some rules you can try out if you wish.
If your composition is in a certain key, let’s say C major for an example, you have certain chords that probably sound nice on the track. Here’s a table with some jazzy chords that should play along with each other quite nicely. If you want you can look more in to chord progressions here: lotusmusic.com/chord-progression-map.html
Common chord progressions in major I - IV - V I - VI - IV - V II - V - I
Major key I II III IV V VI
C chord(:C, :major7) chord(:D, :minor7) chord(:E, :minor7) chord(:F, :major7) chord(:G, "7") chord(:A, :minor7)
Db chord(:Db, :major7) chord(:Eb, :minor7) chord(:F, :minor7) chord(:Gb, :major7) chord(:Ab, "7") chord(:Bb, :minor7)
D chord(:D, :major7) chord(:E, :minor7) chord(:Gb, :minor7) chord(:G, :major7) chord(:A, "7") chord(:B, :minor7)
Eb chord(:Eb, :major7) chord(:F, :minor7) chord(:G, :minor7) chord(:Ab, :major7) chord(:Bb, "7") chord(:C, :minor7)
E chord(:E, :major7) chord(:Gb, :minor7) chord(:Ab, :minor7) chord(:A, :major7) chord(:B, "7") chord(:Db, :minor7)
F chord(:F, :major7) chord(:G, :minor7) chord(:A, :minor7) chord(:Bb, :major7) chord(:C, "7") chord(:D, :minor7)
Gb chord(:Gb, :major7) chord(:Ab, :minor7) chord(:Bb, :minor7) chord(:Cb, :major7) chord(:Db, "7") chord(:Eb, :minor7)
G chord(:G, :major7) chord(:A, :minor7) chord(:B, :minor7) chord(:C, :major7) chord(:D, "7") chord(:E, :minor7)
Ab chord(:Ab, :major7) chord(:Bb, :minor7) chord(:C, :minor7) chord(:Db, :major7) chord(:Eb, "7") chord(:F, :minor7)
A chord(:A, :major7) chord(:B, :minor7) chord(:Db, :minor7) chord(:D, :major7) chord(:E, "7") chord(:Gb, :minor7)
Bb chord(:Bb, :major7) chord(:C, :minor7) chord(:D, :minor7) chord(:Eb, :major7) chord(:F, "7") chord(:G, :minor7)
B chord(:B, :major7) chord(:Db, :minor7) chord(:Eb, :minor7) chord(:E, :major7) chord(:Gb, "7") chord(:Ab, :minor7)
Common chord progressions in natural minor I - VI - VII I - IV - VII I - IV - V I - VI - III - VII II - V - I
Minor key I II III IV V VI VII
Cm chord(:C, :minor7) chord(:D, "m7-5") chord(:Eb, :major7) chord(:F, :minor7) chord(:G, :minor7) chord(:Ab, :major7) chord(:Bb, "7")
Ddm chord(:Dd, :minor7) chord(:Eb, "m7-5") chord(:E, :major7) chord(:Gb, :minor7) chord(:Ab, :minor7) chord(:A, :major7) chord(:B, "7")
Dm chord(:D, :minor7) chord(:E, "m7-5") chord(:F, :major7) chord(:G, :minor7) chord(:A, :minor7) chord(:Bb, :major7) chord(:C, "7")
Ebm chord(:Eb, :minor7) chord(:F, "m7-5") chord(:Gb, :major7) chord(:Ab, :minor7) chord(:Bb, :minor7) chord(:B, :major7) chord(:Db, "7")
Em chord(:E, :minor7) chord(:Gb, "m7-5") chord(:G, :major7) chord(:A, :minor7) chord(:B, :minor7) chord(:C, :major7) chord(:D, "7")
Fm chord(:F, :minor7) chord(:G, "m7-5") chord(:Ab, :major7) chord(:Bb, :minor7) chord(:C, :minor7) chord(:Db, :major7) chord(:Eb, "7")
Gbm chord(:Gb, :minor7) chord(:Ab, "m7-5") chord(:A, :major7) chord(:B, :minor7) chord(:Db, :minor7) chord(:D, :major7) chord(:E, "7")
Gm chord(:G, :minor7) chord(:A, "m7-5") chord(:Bb, :major7) chord(:C, :minor7) chord(:D, :minor7) chord(:Eb, :major7) chord(:F, "7")
Abm chord(:Ab, :minor7) chord(:Bb, "m7-5") chord(:B, :major7) chord(:Db, :minor7) chord(:Eb, :minor7) chord(:E, :major7) chord(:Gb, "7")
Am chord(:A, :minor7) chord(:B, "m7-5") chord(:C, :major7) chord(:D, :minor7) chord(:E, :minor7) chord(:F, :major7) chord(:G, "7")
Bbm chord(:Bb, :minor7) chord(:C, "m7-5") chord(:Db, :major7) chord(:Eb, :minor7) chord(:F, :minor7) chord(:Gb, :major7) chord(:Ab, "7")

Language:
Cheatsheet
Play a note
use_bpm 100
# this is a comment
play 50
sleep 1
play :C3
sleep 1
play_pattern_timed [:c2, :d2, :e2, :d2], [0.5, 0.25, 0.75, 0.5]
Loop
live_loop :drums do
sample :drum_heavy_kick
sleep 1
end
2.times do
play_pattern_timed [:E5, :Eb5], [0.25]
end
play_pattern_timed [:e5, :b4, :d5, :c5], [0.25]
play :a4
sleep 1
Synths and options
use_synth :fm
use_transpose 0
use_octave 0
play :c, attack: 1, decay: 0, sustain: 0, release: 1, amp: 0.5, pan: rrand(-0.5,0.5)
Samples and options
sample :bd_haus, amp: 0.5
sleep 1
sample :drum_cymbal_open, attack: 0.01, sustain: 0, release: 0.1
sleep 1
live_loop :amen_break do
sample :loop_amen, beat_stretch: 2, rate: -1
sleep 2
end
Randomization
rrand(60, 110)
if one_in(6)
# do something
else
# do something else
end
sleep [0, 1, 2].choose
play [:c, :e, :g].choose
FX
with_fx :reverb, mix: 0.5 do
# do something
end
Scales and chords
scale(:c2, :major) # ring of :c2, :d2, :e2, :f2, :g2, :a2, :b2
chord(:c2, :major, , num_octaves: 2) # ring of :c2, :e2, :g2 :c3, :e3, :g3
Tick, ring and variables
play scale(:e3, :minor_pentatonic).tick, release: 0.1
play [:c, :e, :d, :f].ring.tick
r = [0.25, 0.25, 0.5, 1].choose
play chord(:c, :minor).choose, attack: 0, release: r
sleep r
chords = [chord(:C, :minor7), chord(:Ab, :major7)].ring # a ring of chords
c = chords.tick # save next chord to a variable 'c'
c[0] # get the first note of the chord
Other
If your code is getting slow, try use_debug false in the beginning of your code. This reduces the log messages Sonic Pi generates and can speed up the code.

Band Template 1
Load the code as a text file
# Band Example 1
# Mehackit 2016
use_bpm 120
live_loop :rummut do
sample :drum_heavy_kick
sleep 1
sample :drum_snare_hard
sleep 1
sample :drum_heavy_kick
sleep 1
sample :drum_snare_hard
sleep 1
end
live_loop :hihat do
sample :drum_cymbal_closed
sleep 0.25
sample :drum_cymbal_pedal
sleep 1
end
live_loop :basso do
use_synth :fm
play :c2, attack: 0, release: 0.25
sleep 0.25
play :c2, attack: 0, release: 0.3
sleep 2
play :e2
sleep 0.75
play :f2
sleep 1
end
live_loop :melodia do
play_pattern_timed [:c4, :e4, :f4, :g4, :f4, :e4, :f4, :g4, :f4, :e4, :f4], [0.25, 0.25, 0.25, 1.5, 0.25, 0.25, 0.25, 0.25, 0.25, 0.25], attack: 0, release: 0.2
end

Band Template 2
Load the code as a text file
# Band Example 2
# Mehackit 2016
use_bpm 120
live_loop :rummut do
sample :drum_heavy_kick
sleep 1
sample :drum_snare_hard
sleep 1
sample :drum_heavy_kick
sleep 1
sample :drum_snare_hard
sleep 1
end
live_loop :hihat do
sample :drum_cymbal_closed
sleep 0.25
sample :drum_cymbal_pedal
sleep 1
end
live_loop :basso do
use_synth :tb303
play chord(:C2, :major).choose, release: 0.125, cutoff: rrand(60, 110)
sleep 0.25
end
live_loop :melodia do
if one_in(6)
use_transpose 2
else
use_transpose 0
end
play_pattern_timed [:c4, :e4, :f4, :g4, :f4, :e4, :f4, :g4, :f4, :e4, :f4], [0.25, 0.25, 0.25, 1.5, 0.25, 0.25, 0.25, 0.25, 0.25, 0.25], attack: 0, release: 0.2
end

Simple Slow Techno
Load the code as a text file
# Simple Slow Techno
# Mehackit 2016
use_bpm 80
live_loop :kickdrum do
sample :bd_haus, amp: 1
sleep 1
end
live_loop :hihat do
sample :drum_cymbal_soft, amp: 0.4
sleep 0.5
end
live_loop :snare do
sleep 1
sample :drum_snare_soft, amp: 1.3
sleep 1
end
live_loop :pulputus do
with_fx :reverb, amp: 1, mix: 0.25 do
use_synth :tb303
nuotti = (chord :C2, :minor).choose
play nuotti, cutoff: rrand(10, 130), release: 0.1, amp: 0.7, release: 0.1
sleep 0.25
end
end

Hip Hop Beat
Load the code as a text file
# Hip Hop Beat
# Mehackit 2016
use_bpm 90
live_loop :biitti do
sample :bd_808, rate: 1, amp: 4
sleep 1
sample :elec_hi_snare, amp: 1
sleep 1
sample :bd_808, rate: 1, amp: 4
sleep 1
sample :elec_hi_snare, amp: 1
sleep 1
end
live_loop :luuppi do
sample :loop_breakbeat, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 4
end
live_loop :kitaramelodia do
sample :guit_e_fifths, rate: 0.5, amp: 1.5
sample :guit_e_fifths, rate: 1, amp: 0.8
sleep 1.5
sample :guit_e_fifths, rate: 1.5, amp: 0.8
sleep 1.5
sample :guit_e_fifths, rate: 1.4, amp: 0.8
sleep 3
sample :guit_e_slide, rate: 1, amp: 0.8
sleep 2
end
live_loop :ujellus do
with_fx :echo, phase: 1.5, mix: 0.5 do
use_synth :mod_beep
use_synth_defaults mod_phase: 0.125, pulse_width: 0.8, mod_wave: 2, attack: 1
play :G5
sleep 8
end
end
live_loop :hihat do
16.times do
sample :drum_cymbal_pedal, start: 0.05, finish: 0.4, rate: 3, amp: 0.5 + rrand(-0.1, 0.1)
sleep 0.125
end
4.times do
sample :drum_cymbal_pedal, start: 0.05, finish: 0.6, rate: 3, amp: 0.5 + rrand(-0.1, 0.1)
sleep 0.25
end
16.times do
sample :drum_cymbal_pedal, start: 0.1, finish: 0.3, rate: 3, amp: 0.5 + rrand(-0.1, 0.1)
sleep 0.0625
end
end

Language:
Play Pattern Fun
Load the code as a text file
# Having Fun with the Play Pattern Timed command
# Mehackit 2016
use_bpm 100
live_loop :kick do
sample :bd_haus
sleep(1)
end
live_loop :melodia do
with_fx :reverb, mix: 0.4 do
use_synth :piano
play chord(:E4, :minor7), release: 2, amp: 2
use_synth :tri
play_pattern_timed chord(:E3, :minor7), 0.25, pan: rrand(-1, 1), release: 0.05
play_pattern_timed chord(:E4, :minor7), 0.25, pan: rrand(-1, 1), release: 0.1
play_pattern_timed chord(:E5, :minor7), 0.25, pan: rrand(-1, 1), release: 0.2
play_pattern_timed chord(:E6, :minor7), 0.25, pan: rrand(-1, 1), release: 0.3
use_synth :piano
play chord(:C4, :major7), release: 2, amp: 2
use_synth :saw
play_pattern_timed chord(:C3, :major7), 0.25, pan: rrand(-1, 1), release: 0.05
play_pattern_timed chord(:C4, :major7), 0.25, pan: rrand(-1, 1), release: 0.1
play_pattern_timed chord(:C5, :major7), 0.25, pan: rrand(-1, 1), release: 0.2
play_pattern_timed chord(:C6, :major7), 0.25, pan: rrand(-1, 1), release: 0.3
end
end
live_loop :hihats do
sleep 0.5
with_fx :reverb, mix: 0.3 do
3.times do
sample :drum_cymbal_closed, rate: 1.5, vol: rrand(0.3, 0.7)
sleep 0.5
end
sample :perc_snap , rate: 2, vol: rrand(0.1, 0.3)
end
end

Language:
Laidback Breakbeat
Load the code as a text file
# Laidback breakbeat with live_loop delays and intro part
# Mehackit 2016
use_bpm 120
with_fx :reverb, mix: 0.4 do
with_fx :echo, mix: 0.7 do
sample :ambi_swoosh, amp: 2
sleep 3
sample :ambi_lunar_land, rate: 2
sleep 1
end
end
live_loop :rummut do
sample :bd_klub, amp: 2
sleep 0.5
sample :bd_klub, amp: 2, rate: 1.25
sleep 0.25
sample :bd_ada, amp: 1.5
sleep 0.25
with_fx :reverb, mix: 0.2, decay: 1 do
sample :sn_dolf, amp: 1.75
end
sleep 0.25
sample :bd_pure, amp: 1.5
sleep 0.75
sample :bd_ada, amp: 2
sleep 1
with_fx :reverb, mix: 0.2, decay: 1 do
sample :drum_snare_soft, amp: 2
end
sleep 1
end
live_loop :bassolinja1, delay: 32 do
use_synth :tb303
use_synth_defaults release: 0.125 + rrand(0, 0.2), amp: 0.9, pan: rrand(-0.5, 0.5)
use_transpose -12
use_transpose 0 if one_in(3)
n = (ring :r, :r, :d2, :d3, :f3, :r, :d1, :f2).tick
play n, cutoff: rrand(30, 130)
sleep 0.25
end
live_loop :bassolinja2, delay: 32 do
use_synth :saw
use_synth_defaults release: rrand(0.1, 0.3), amp: 0.8, pan: rrand(-0.5, 0.5)
use_transpose 0
use_transpose 12 if one_in(3)
with_fx :bitcrusher, mix: 0.5 do
with_fx :echo, mix: 0.6 do
n = (ring :r, :d2, :d3, :f3, :r, :d1, :f2, :r).tick
play n, cutoff: rrand(70, 120)
end
end
sleep 0.25
end
live_loop :pädit do
use_synth :prophet
use_synth_defaults cutoff: rrand(70, 110), release: rrand(1, 4), amp: 1
with_fx :panslicer, mix: 0.5 do
with_fx :hpf, cutoff: 70 do
with_fx :reverb, mix: 0.4 do
with_fx :echo, mix: 0.2 do
2.times do
play_chord chord(:D3, :minor )
sleep 0.75
end
sleep 0.5
play_chord chord(:D4, :minor ), attack: 4, release: 2
sleep 2
end
end
end
end
end
live_loop :hihat, delay: 32 do
with_fx :flanger, mix: 0 do
sample :perc_snap, amp: rrand(0.9, 1.4), rate: rrand(1.9, 2.1), cutoff: rrand(80, 120), pan: rrand(-0.25, 0.25)
end
sleep 0.25
end
2.times do
3.times do
sample :loop_amen, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 4
end
sample :loop_amen_full, beat_stretch: 16, start: 0.75
sleep 4
end

# Dubstep bass and beat programming with the "at" command
# Mehackit 2016
use_bpm 140
use_debug false
basari = :bd_klub
virveli = :sn_dolf
haitsu = :perc_snap
haitsu2 = :drum_cymbal_pedal
with_fx :distortion, mix: 0.08 do
with_fx :nrhpf, mix: 0.05 do
live_loop :drumloop do
at [1, 2, 4] do
sample basari, amp: rrand(1, 1.5), rate: rrand(0.95, 1.05)
end
at [2.5, 6.5] do
sample virveli, amp: rrand(0.6, 1), rate: rrand(0.95, 1.05)
sleep 0.1
with_fx :gverb, amp: 0.6, mix: 1, spread: 1, delay: 10 do
sample virveli, amp: rrand(0.3, 0.5), rate: rrand(0.95, 1.05)
end
end
at [0.5, 1.5, 2.5, 3.5, 4.5, 5.5, 6.5, 7.5] do
with_fx :flanger, mix: 0.5, depth: 40, delay: 20, decay: 5, feedback: rrand(0.1, 1.0) do
sample haitsu, amp: rrand(0.3, 0.5), rate: rrand(2.1, 2.2), pan: rrand(-0.25, 0.25)
if one_in(10)
sleep 0.5
sample haitsu2, amp: rrand(0.6, 0.9), rate: rrand(1.1, 1.2), pan: rrand(-0.25, 0.25)
end
end
end
sleep 8
end
live_loop :bassolinjaus do
with_fx :distortion, mix: 0.5, amp: 0.95 do
3.times do
at [0.5, 2.5, 4.5] do
with_fx :panslicer, smooth_up: 0.1, smooth_down: 0.1 do
sample :bass_dnb_f, pitch: 0, finish: 0.6
end
end
at [6.5] do
with_fx :panslicer, smooth_up: 0.1, smooth_down: 0.1 do
sample :bass_dnb_f, pitch: 0, finish: 0.6
end
end
sleep 8
end
n = 1
at [0.5, 2.5, 4.5, 6.5] do
with_fx :panslicer, mix: 1, wave: 3 do
with_fx :reverb, mix: 0.6, room: 0.2, amp: 2 do
7.times do
sample :bass_dnb_f, rate: n, finish: 0.1
n += 0.15
sleep 0.25
end
sample :bass_voxy_hit_c, rate: n, amp: 1
end
end
end
sleep 8
end
end
live_loop :randoming_pädi do
kesto = [8, 10, 12].choose
nuku = kesto / 2.0
use_synth :hollow
with_fx :hpf, cutoff: 75 do
with_fx :reverb, room: 0.8, mix: 0.8, amp: 1 do
nuotit = [:F4, :B4, :D4, :F5, :B5, :D5].choose
play nuotit, attack: kesto / 3.0, release: kesto
end
end
sleep nuku
end
end
end

Amen Breakbeat Jingle
Load the code as a text file
# Amen Breakbeat Jingle
# Mehackit 2016
use_bpm 160
with_fx :nrlpf, mix: 0.5, res: 0.4, mix: 1.0, cutoff: 60, cutoff_slide: 4, mix_slide: 4 do |dc|
with_fx :bitcrusher, mix: 0.2, sample_rate: 1000, sample_rate_slide: 4, mix_slide: 4 do |bc|
control bc, sample_rate_slide: 4, sample_rate: 22000
control dc, cutoff_slide: 4, cutoff: 130
with_fx :wobble, mix: 1, res: 0.8, phase: 0.3, phase_slide: 8 do |w|
control w, phase: 10
r = sample :loop_amen_full, rate: 1.5, rate_slide: 8, cutoff_slide: 8, cutoff: 130
control r, rate: 0.5, cutoff: 30
sleep 8
end
with_fx :reverb, room: 0.9 do
sample :ambi_piano
end
sample :loop_amen, rate: 1, beat_stretch: 4
control bc, sample_rate_slide: 4, sample_rate: 10000
control dc, cutoff_slide: 2, cutoff: 130
sleep 4
sample :loop_amen, start: 0.0, finish: 0.175, rate: 1, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 0.5
sample :loop_amen, start: 0.0, finish: 0.175, rate: 1.1, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 0.5
sample :loop_amen, start: 0.75, finish: 0.875, rate: 1, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 0.5
sample :loop_amen, start: 0.75, finish: 0.875, rate: 1, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 0.5
control dc, cutoff_slide: 4, cutoff: 70
sample :loop_amen, start: 0.5, finish: 0.75, rate: 1, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 1
sample :loop_amen, start: 0.5, finish: 0.75, rate: 0.95, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 1
sample :loop_amen, start: 0.5, finish: 0.75, rate: 0.9, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 1
sample :loop_amen, start: 0.5, finish: 0.75, rate: 0.85, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 1
control bc, sample_rate_slide: 0, sample_rate: 1000
control dc, cutoff_slide: 0, cutoff: 70
sample :loop_amen, rate: 1.25, start: 0, finish: 0.25, beat_stretch: 4
control bc, sample_rate_slide: 4, sample_rate: 10000
control dc, cutoff_slide: 1, cutoff: 130
sample :loop_amen, rate: 1.0, start: 0.25, finish: 0.35, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 0.25
sample :loop_amen, rate: 1.05, start: 0.25, finish: 0.35, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 0.25
sample :loop_amen, rate: 1.1, start: 0.25, finish: 0.35, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 0.25
sample :loop_amen, rate: 1.15, start: 0.25, finish: 0.35, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 0.25
sample :loop_amen, rate: 1.20, start: 0.25, finish: 0.35, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 0.25
sample :loop_amen, rate: 1.25, start: 0.25, finish: 0.35, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 0.25
sample :loop_amen, rate: 1.3, start: 0.25, finish: 0.35, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 0.25
sample :loop_amen, rate: 1.35, start: 0.25, finish: 0.35, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 0.25
sample :loop_amen, rate: 1.65, start: 0, finish: 0.15, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 0.5
sample :loop_amen, rate: 1.25, start: 0, finish: 0.25, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 0.5
sample :loop_amen, start: 0.75, finish: 0.875, rate: 1, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 0.5
sample :loop_amen, start: 0.75, finish: 0.875, rate: 1, beat_stretch: 4
sleep 0.5
with_fx :reverb, room: 0.9 do
sample :loop_amen_full, start: 0.9, finish: 1, rate: 1, beat_stretch: 16, amp: 0.8
sleep 0.5
sample :ambi_piano, rate: 0.75
end
sleep 8
control dc, mix: 0.0
control bc, mix: 0.0
end

00. SONIC_PI_environment_rules.txt
Source guide
This technical guide details the mandatory constraints and critical stability issues when running Sonic Pi v4.5.1 on Windows 10. A major focus is placed on the very specific vocabulary required for chord qualities, emphasizing that many standard music abbreviations are unsupported and must be replaced with the exact recognized symbols like :major7 or :m7. Furthermore, the guide warns of common audio crackling and timing drift due to CPU overload when employing heavy synths, frequent FX switching, or long-release notes within fast loops. Ultimately, the document serves to offer practical workarounds—such as favoring lightweight synths and pre-calculating randomized patterns—enabling users to write complex, reliable code despite the environment’s technical limitations.
ENVIRONMENT RULES (Windows 10 version 22H2 / Sonic Pi Version 4.5.1 ‘8oh8’)
Absolutely — here’s the *real* situation with Sonic Pi’s chord-naming conventions as they exist in **v4.5.1 on Windows 10**, and why you’re seeing those errors.
## ✔️ What Sonic Pi Actually Accepts as Chord Qualities
Sonic Pi’s `chord` function uses a **very specific vocabulary**. Most errors come from using names that *look* standard (like :maj7 or :Gmajor) but **aren’t defined** in Sonic Pi’s internal lookup table.
Here is the authoritative list of chord names that *your* Sonic Pi version recognizes:
### **🎵 Triads**
* `:major`, `:M`, `:maj`
* `:minor`, `:m`, `:min`
* `:aug`
* `:dim`
* `:sus2`
* `:sus4`
### **🎵 Seventh Chords**
* `:dom7` *(the proper name for a standard 7th)*
(You CANNOT just write `:7` in your version.)
* `:major7`, `:M7`
* `:minor7`, `:m7`
* `:half_diminished7`, `:m7b5`
* `:dim7`
### **🎵 Extended / Altered (only the ones Sonic Pi v4.5 actually ships with)**
* `:dom9`
* `:major9`
* `:minor9`
* `:dom11`
* `:major11`
* `:minor11`
### **🎵 Power Chord**
* `:power` (root + fifth)
---
## ✔️ What Sonic Pi **does NOT** understand in your version
These will throw errors:
❌ `:7`
❌ `:maj7` *(looks right but unsupported — must be :major7 or :M7)*
❌ `:Gmajor`
❌ `:Amaj7` as a single symbol
❌ Case-dependent note names inside chord function
❌ Adding “3” or “4” to chord qualities (like `:G3major`, etc.)
---
## ✔️ What Sonic Pi *does* want
Chord root and chord quality are always separate:
```ruby
chord(:A3, :m7)
chord(:F3, :M7)
chord(:B3, :dim)
chord(:E3, :minor7) # or :m7
```
Case-insensitive for note names (`:a3`, `:A3` both work),
BUT chord qualities are exact matches (`:maj7` does NOT exist, only `:major7` or `:M7`).
---
## ✔️ Your custom chord ring — corrected for Sonic Pi 4.5.1
Using correct quality names:
```ruby
set :custom_chords,
(ring chord(:A3, :m7),
chord(:F3, :M7),
chord(:B3, :dim),
chord(:E3, :m7))
```
---
## ✔️ If you want, I can rewrite your melody-writer script to:
* use only supported chord types
* auto-clean any illegal chord names you pass in
* throw friendly errors when you feed it something Sonic Pi doesn’t support
* simplify custom chord workflows
Just say the word and paste whatever version you're working with.
Good question — there *are* a handful of known caveats, quirks, and “gotchas” when running Sonic Pi v4.5.1 on Windows 10 (22H2). These don’t make it unusable — but if you know them, you can write scripts more reliably and avoid frustrating bugs. Below are the most important ones to keep in mind, along with practical guidance.
---
# ⚠️ Known Windows-10 + Sonic Pi 4.5.1 Issues & What to Watch Out For
## • Chord naming & quality support is limited
As we discussed — not all chord-quality names are valid. Use only those officially supported (`:major`, `:m7`, `:dim`, etc.). Unsupported names (e.g. `:maj7`, `:7`, `:Gmajor`) will cause errors or silent failures.
## • Audio driver & output latency + crackling is common
Many Windows users report **audio crackling or glitches**, even on modest synth usage, when using built-in Realtek or generic sound cards. ([in_thread][1])
* Often a **driver issue** — updating your audio driver helps.
* Running other audio-heavy apps in background (Discord, browsers, video apps) may introduce dropouts.
* Lowering synth/polyphony count, using lighter synths, or reducing sustain/decay times helps reduce load and glitches.
## • Heavy chords & long-release notes in fast loops overload CPU / cause timing drift
Because 4.5.1’s audio engine (and Ruby underpinnings) on Windows is somewhat resource-hungry:
* If you play large chords (e.g., 6–8 voices) with long release inside rapid loops, performance can degrade quickly. Community reports of “timing desync” or “crash after a few seconds” are common. ([in_thread][2])
* Avoid stacking heavy synths + heavy FX + long release in fast sequences.
## • Only one instance of Sonic Pi can run reliably at a time
As of v4.3 (on which 4.5 builds post-date), the GUI blocks opening more than one instance per session to avoid audio-server conflicts. ([New Releases][3])
* If you try to open a second window while another is already running, it will quit silently.
* So for testing multiple scripts: use buffer tabs — don’t open multiple Sonic Pi executables.
## • Log files and error messages location can be confusing
If Sonic Pi fails to boot or audio server fails, the error logs matter, but on Windows 10 they may be obscure. Community documentation says: logs live under `C:\Users\\.sonic-pi\log`, or in UI popups when there’s a startup error. ([GitHub Wiki See][4])
* When debugging timing errors or crashes, check those logs first before blaming your code.
## • Some newer synth names / features / sample-stretch options introduced after 4.5.1 may not exist
Because 4.5.1 was released some time ago (see release history) and later versions added features and synths, code from modern examples may use synths or opts that 4.5.1 doesn’t recognise. ([GitHub][5])
* Always check if a synth or function exists before using — test minimal example first.
## • Changing `use_synth`, heavy FX, or frequent sample triggering can destabilize timing
Rapid context switching in synth or FX layers increases CPU overhead on Windows, which can introduce timing jitter or dropped notes after a running script for a minute or two. Reports in forums attribute this to Windows scheduling and audio driver buffer mis-management. ([in_thread][2])
---
# 🔧 Practical Workarounds & Best Practices for Windows 10 + Sonic Pi
Given the above issues — here’s how I recommend structuring your projects (and what to watch out for) to maximize stability and minimize pain:
| Problem / Risk | Recommended Practice |
| ----------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Chord naming errors | Only use chord qualities supported in 4.5.1 (see list earlier). Validate any new code you copy/paste. |
| Audio crackle / latency | Update audio drivers; close other audio apps; use lighter synths (e.g. `:blade`, `:pluck`, `:dsaw`), reduce polyphony and release times; avoid long sustained chords in fast loops. |
| CPU overload / timing drift | Limit number of concurrent live_loops; avoid stacking heavy synths + effects; use simpler synths; pre-calc patterns instead of dynamic random-heavy loops if runtime matters. |
| Multiple instances causing conflict | Use buffer tabs inside one Sonic Pi window for different scripts. Don’t open multiple windows. |
| Hidden errors / crashes | When something fails: check `~/.sonic-pi/log` folder or startup error popups. Use debug mode only when needed. |
| Feature mismatches (new synths / options) | Test new code snippet alone; check documentation; search for synth name before using. |
| Frequent `use_synth` or FX switching | Normalize synth/FX usage: set synth once per live_loop; avoid switching inside tight loops. |
| Long-release chords in loops | Prefer shorter releases/sustains, or spread chords over longer time spans. |
---
# 🧑‍🔧 My Recommendations for **YOU** (Given Your Usage & Interests)
Since you’re doing complex generative music, lots of chord/melody experimentation, and likely layering many voices:
1. **Favor lightweight synths** — e.g. `:pluck`, `:blade`, `:dsaw`, `:pretty_bell`. Use heavy ones (`:piano`) sparingly and only for important moments.
2. **Keep chord voicings modest** — 3–4 notes max where possible; avoid 7+ note stacked chords if you plan to loop rapidly.
3. **Avoid FX stacking** — Sizable reverbs or heavy filters can destabilize the audio engine over time. Use simple FX or apply globally sparingly.
4. **Use “pre-calc” loops for repeated structure + randomness** — instead of lots of random choices every tick, pre-generate patterns (e.g. random melody arrays, chord sequences) before entering the main live loop.
5. **Check audio settings** — if you use Realtek onboard audio, try using “Stereo” rather than “Spatial / Surround,” disable audio enhancements, and ensure Windows isn’t doing “exclusive mode” tricks.
6. **If using external USB audio interface** — make sure drivers are up-to-date, and select it inside Windows before launching Sonic Pi. Avoid switching audio devices mid-song.
7. **Use small buffers** — avoid super-long sustain / release in loops; this reduces risk of audio dropouts accumulating.
---
# 🧠 In Short
* Yes — there *are* limitations and quirks with Windows + 4.5.1.
* None of them are show-stoppers — but they shape **how you should write code** to avoid trouble.
* With awareness and discipline (light synths, modest voicings, modest FX, clean loops), you can reliably write **complex, generative, musically rich code**.
* If you hit frequent crackling or dropouts, assume it’s the engine + OS layers, not your musical logic — then simplify or optimize accordingly.
---

That way, **every script you start from will already be optimized for 4.5.1 on Windows**. Want me to put that together for you now?

Yes — beyond the major Windows/4.5.1 quirks we already covered, there are a few subtler but important things that can bite you while scripting in Sonic Pi on Windows. Most of these are about timing, resource management, and syntax pitfalls specific to your version:
---
## 1. **Timing and sleep accuracy**
* `sleep` isn’t perfectly precise — especially when many live_loops or threads are running. On Windows, heavy CPU load or multiple live_loops can cause drift over time.
* **Workarounds:**
* Keep loops simple and avoid deeply nested threads if possible.
* Pre-calculate patterns rather than generating random notes every tick inside fast loops.
* Use `sync:` when coordinating multiple loops — it’s more reliable than trying to `sleep` exactly in sync manually.
---
## 2. **Live_loop design**
* Avoid having too many `live_loop`s playing simultaneously — each loop adds CPU overhead, especially if they use long `release` notes, multiple synths, or FX.
* Use `in_thread` inside a loop to layer sounds instead of creating a separate live_loop if it’s just for one-time events.
---
## 3. **FX usage**
* Each FX (`reverb`, `echo`, `compressor`, etc.) adds CPU load. Applying FX inside fast loops or on heavy chords can create stutter or skipped notes.
* **Tip:** Wrap multiple notes in one FX block instead of calling FX for each note separately.
```ruby
with_fx :reverb, room: 0.8 do
play_chord chord(:c3, :m7), release: 2
play_pattern_timed [:e3, :g3], 0.25
end
```
---
## 4. **Chord and note naming**
* Stick to the chord names and qualities that your version recognizes:
* Roots: `:c3`, `:d4`, `:g5`, etc.
* Qualities: `:major`, `:minor`, `:m7`, `:dim`
* Avoid upper-case `:M7` vs `:maj7` inconsistencies — pick one that works consistently in your 4.5.1 installation.
---
## 5. **Array and ring usage**
* Rings (`(ring ...)`) and arrays (`[...]`) behave differently in some contexts:
* Rings are great for looping through elements cyclically, but you still need to extract chords carefully.
* Nested structures (e.g., `[(ring chord(:a, :m7), chord(:c, :M7))]`) can cause errors if you try to `play_chord` directly — you may need to flatten them or iterate through the inner rings.
---
## 6. **Synth switching**
* Frequent `use_synth` changes mid-loop can slightly disrupt timing, especially on Windows.
* **Tip:** Keep one synth per live_loop if possible, or switch only between loops.
---
## 7. **Randomness**
* Using `choose`, `rand`, or probabilistic triggers heavily inside loops is fun, but:
* Can cause uneven timing if the loop sleeps less or more than expected.
* Pre-generating random sequences often produces more reliable timing.
---
## 8. **Global state**
* Variables like `set`/`get` are fine for controlling loops, but excessive reliance can slow performance and increase the chance of race conditions if many threads are writing/reading simultaneously.
* For large generative systems, consider pre-defining all random sequences in arrays and only using `set` for structural flags like `:song_status`.
---
## 9. **Windows-specific path issues**
* If loading external samples, be careful with backslashes (`\`) vs forward slashes (`/`) in file paths. Sonic Pi on Windows tolerates forward slashes better:
```ruby
sample "C:/Users/Nathan/Music/my_sample.wav"
```
* Avoid very long file paths — Sonic Pi’s sampler can fail silently.
---
## 10. **Polyphony and sustain management**
* Long-release notes combined with overlapping live_loops can max out CPU, especially on Windows with lower-latency audio drivers.
* Use `release` and `amp` judiciously. Sometimes cutting sustain in half and layering notes via `sleep` produces a cleaner sound and reduces crashes.
---
✅ **Summary**
* Keep live_loops simple and lightweight.
* Limit FX and long-release chords in fast loops.
* Stick to compatible chord naming.
* Pre-generate randomness when possible.
* Be careful with rings, nested arrays, and synth switching.
* Watch file paths and system resources.
---

* Proper chord naming
* Lightweight synth + FX defaults
* Pre-generated random sequences for reliable timing
* Correct handling of custom chord arrays and rings

---
## ✅ What chord / chord‑type names *are* supported in Sonic Pi (≈ v4.5.1)
* Basic triads & extended chords: e.g.
* `chord :E3, :minor`
* `chord :E3, :m7`
* `chord :E3, :dim7`
* `chord :E3, :dom7` (i.e. dominant‑7) ([Sonic Pi][2])
* Common shorthand for triads and sevenths seems to use “:major”, “:minor”, “:m7”, “:dim7”, “:dom7” etc. (not “:M7” or “:7” with text—though you might see “’7’” as a string in some community code, more on that below). ([Sonic Pi][2])
* Arpeggios: after generating a chord with `chord`, you can feed it to `play_pattern`, `play_pattern_timed`, or iterate and `play` each note. ([Sonic Pi][2])
**Importantly:** Sonic Pi docs explicitly list examples like `chord :E3, :m7`, `chord :E3, :minor`, `chord :E3, :dim7`, `chord :E3, :dom7`. ([Sonic Pi][2])
---
## ⚠️ What *doesn't* reliably work (or isn’t guaranteed) — based on docs + community experience
* Using `:maj7`, `:M7`, or `:7` as chord‑type symbols — these are **not** guaranteed to work. The official docs do *not* list them as valid examples. ([Sonic Pi][2])
* Using arbitrary strings (or exotic chord‑extensions like “:maj9”, “:11”, “:13”, etc.) — support seems limited and often community experiments or custom workarounds pick up those chords, not guaranteed by Sonic Pi core. ([sonic-pi.mehackit.org][3])
* Mixing chord‑type naming conventions (like combining symbol + string, or unusual punctuation) — that tends to produce syntax or runtime errors.
In fact, in community discussion, people often fallback to explicit note‑lists or simple chord types when extended chords are needed, or build “compound” chords manually rather than rely on ambiguous chord name syntax. ([in_thread][4])
---
## 🎯 What you should do in your version (4.5.1 on Windows 10)
* Prefer the “safe” chord‑type keywords documented: `:major`, `:minor`, `:m7`, `:dim7`, `:dom7`.
* Avoid using `:maj7`, `"7"`, `:M7`, or other non‑standard chord names — those are risky.
* If you need more complex chords (9ths, 11ths, altered chords), you may need to build them manually — e.g. using `chord(:root, :major)` then adding extra notes with `+ []` or constructing a numeric note‑list explicitly.
* For playing a chord, use `play chord(...)`, or expand to an arpeggio with `play_pattern` or `play_pattern_timed`; do *not* assume more advanced chord‑name support.
---
## 💡 Additional “gotchas” / general scripting warnings for your setup
* Many Sonic Pi community scripts use naming conventions or chord shortcuts that pre‑date standardization — that’s why you saw `:M7`, `:7`, etc. in some examples, but those may not work reliably in newer versions.
* There is no documented “list of all symbolic chord‑types” built into accessible global symbols; what exists are doc‑lists like in the “Chords” page. ([SunderB][5])
* If you attempt to create a chord with an unrecognized chord‑type name, Sonic Pi will raise a syntax or runtime error — which seems to be what you experienced.
* For complex or experimental harmonic ideas (extended chords, non‑standard voicings), manual note‑list construction is often safer/reliable than trying to rely on shorthand chord names.
---
## ✅ My recommendation for you — the “best practices” going forward
Given your history of experimentation and complexity, here’s what I suggest as a stable “chord‑naming policy” for your future Sonic Pi scripts:
* **Default to:** `:major`, `:minor`, `:m7`, `:dim7`, `:dom7` for chord building.
* **Manually build extended/altered chords** when you need non‑standard harmonies. For example:
```ruby
# A “custom” chord: root, third, fifth, plus an added 9th
base = chord :A3, :minor
extra = (note :B4) # or just an integer midi note
play (base + [extra])
```
* **Test chord generation quick**: put a small snippet at the top of your script that `puts chord(:C3, :major)`, `puts chord(:E3, :dim7)`, etc. — to make sure syntax is acceptable before embedding into bigger loops.
* **Use arpeggios or note‑list expansions** when you want complex voicings or layering — arpeggios give you more timing control, layering gives you independent voice management.

This comprehensive guide introduces Sonic Pi, a code-based music creation platform developed by Sam Aaron that bridges the gap between programming and composition. The text explains how users can generate sounds using synths and samples, manipulating them through parameters like amplitude, panning, and ADSR envelopes. It highlights the platform's core strength, live coding, which allows performers to modify scripts in real-time using live loops and concurrency through threading. Advanced concepts are also covered, including randomization for variety, the use of Studio FX, and organizing musical data within rings and scales. Finally, the guide details how to maintain deterministic synchronization across multiple threads using the Time State system.

Ok, I'd like you to make a general Sonic Pi script popular song skeleton that uses traditional instrumentation, follows standard music advice and has all the basic components: rhythm (2-bar repeat, with variation), chord progression (4-bars per chord, with variations and smart transitions), and a nice melody line (8-bars, with smart melody-chord anchoring to create emotional motion).

This Sonic Pi script skeleton provides the requested structure for a popular song, utilizing basic components, traditional sound elements, and programming techniques like live_loop and Time State synchronization to manage rhythm, harmony, and melody interaction
.
I have set the tempo to 120 Beats Per Minute (BPM)
. The composition is structured around a four-chord progression in C Major (I-vi-IV-V), with each chord held for 4 bars (16 beats) to create a slow harmonic rhythm
.
Sonic Pi Popular Song Skeleton
use_bpm 120
use_debug false # Recommended for performance stability [6]

# --- 1. HARMONIC STRUCTURE SETUP (Chord Progression) ---

# I - vi - IV - V progression in C Major. 
# Using "safe" chord qualities compatible with Sonic Pi 4.5.1 [7].
# The ring repeats every 4 bars * 4 chords = 16 bars (64 beats) cycle.
chords = (ring 
  chord(:C3, :major7), # I - C Major 7th
  chord(:A3, :minor7),  # vi - A Minor 7th
  chord(:F3, :major7), # IV - F Major 7th
  chord(:G3, :dom7)    # V - G Dominant 7th
)

# Initialize global Time State to share the current chord across loops [2, 6].
set :current_chord, chords


# --- 2. CHORD PROGRESSION LOOP (4 Bars per Chord) ---

# This loop updates the current harmonic context every 16 beats (4 bars) [8].
live_loop :chords do
  # Get the next chord from the progression ring [9]
  current_c = chords.tick 
  set :current_chord, current_c # Store chord in global state [2]
  
  # Play the current chord lightly using a lightweight synth [10].
  use_synth :dsaw 
  # Play all notes in the chord list [11]
  play current_c, release: 16, amp: 0.25, pan: 0 # Sustain for the full 4 bars [12]
  
  sleep 16 # Wait 4 bars (16 beats) before moving to the next chord [13]
end


# --- 3. RHYTHM LOOP (2-Bar Repeat with Variation) ---

# Traditional drum samples provide the rhythmic foundation [14].
live_loop :drums do
  # Ensure drums sync to the chord change (every 16 beats) if they fall out of phase [15].
  # We synchronize to the :chords loop start, then break the time down for the drums.
  sync :chords 
  
  # Bar 1 (4 beats)
  sample :drum_heavy_kick, amp: 1.2
  sleep 1
  sample :drum_snare_hard, amp: 0.8
  sleep 1
  sample :drum_heavy_kick, amp: 1.1
  sample :drum_cymbal_closed, pan: rrand(-0.2, 0.2), amp: 0.5 # Variation 1: Snare on 2/4 and kick variation
  sleep 1
  sample :drum_snare_hard, amp: 0.8
  sleep 1
  
  # Bar 2 (4 beats) - Rhythmic Variation
  sample :bd_haus, amp: 1.0 # Use a lighter kick variation on beat 1 [16]
  sleep 0.5
  sample :drum_cymbal_closed, pan: rrand(-0.5, 0.5), amp: 0.4 # Hi-hat on the off-beats [17]
  sleep 0.5
  sample :drum_cymbal_closed, pan: rrand(-0.5, 0.5), amp: 0.4
  sleep 0.5
  sample :drum_cymbal_closed, pan: rrand(-0.5, 0.5), amp: 0.4
  sleep 0.5
  sample :drum_heavy_kick, amp: 1.2
  sleep 1
  sample :drum_snare_hard, amp: 0.8
  sleep 1
  
  # Total sleep is 8 beats (2 bars). This loop naturally repeats this 2-bar pattern.
end

# --- 4. MELODY LOOP (8-Bar Repeat with Anchoring) ---

# The melody repeats an 8-bar (32-beat) phrase. It relies on the :current_chord state for note selection, 
# creating emotional motion by balancing scale tones (tension/movement) and chord tones (resolution/anchoring) [18].
live_loop :melody do
  # Melody syncs its 8-bar phase to the chord progression starting point [15].
  sync :chords
  
  # The melody phrase consists of 8 segments of 4 beats (32 1/4 notes total)
  
  # Retrieve the current chord list (this is the same chord for the next 16 beats)
  # But the progression updates every 16 beats (4 bars), so we must retrieve it every loop segment.
  
  # We use the C Major scale as the base for improvisational notes [19]
  base_scale = (scale :c5, :major, num_octaves: 1).ring 
  
  # Play 8 iterations of 4 steps each (total 32 beats / 8 bars)
  8.times do |i|
    current_c = get[:current_chord] # Anchoring: retrieve the current chord from Time State [20]
    use_synth :pluck # Lightweight synth for traditional lead sound [10]
    use_synth_defaults release: 0.125, attack: 0.01, amp: 1.0 [21, 22]

    # Melodic Phrase (4 beats per iteration/bar, 16th notes feel)
    16.times do # 4 notes per beat * 4 beats = 16 steps per bar
      
      # Smart Anchoring logic: 1/4 probability to hit a strong chord tone (anchor), otherwise use a scale tone (motion) [23]
      n = if one_in(4) 
            current_c.choose # High resolution/consonance [24]
          else
            base_scale.choose # High movement/possible dissonance (emotional motion) [24]
          end

      play n, pan: rrand(-0.1, 0.1)
      sleep 0.25 # Play 16th notes 
    end
  end
end
Explanation of Components
1. Harmony and Progression (4 Bars per Chord, Transitions): The chord progression is defined using a ring of four chords: Cmaj7, Am7, Fmaj7, and Gdom7
. This popular sequence ensures musical cohesion
. The :chords live_loop manages the harmony by using the .tick function to step through the chords ring every 16 beats (4 bars)
. The set :current_chord command places the active chord into the Time State system, making it instantly accessible to all other threads, which allows for "smart transitions" since other elements know the upcoming harmony
.
2. Rhythm (2-Bar Repeat, Variation): The :drums live_loop establishes a basic 4/4 backbeat using traditional samples like :drum_heavy_kick and :drum_snare_hard
. The loop consists of 8 beats (2 bars) and utilizes varying sleep times (e.g., sleep 1, sleep 0.5) and randomized panning (pan: rrand(-0.5, 0.5)) on the hi-hats for variation and texture within the repeating structure
.
3. Melody (8-Bar Repeat, Anchoring): The :melody live_loop defines an 8-bar phrase (32 beats of content) synchronized with the main chord loop via sync :chords
. It implements "smart melody-chord anchoring" by dynamically fetching the current chord list using get[:current_chord]
. Inside the inner loop, it uses the one_in(4) conditional function to decide whether to randomly choose a note strictly from the harmonically consonant current chord (for anchoring) or from the broader C Major scale (for emotional movement and tension)
.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
This structure is like an orchestra where the conductor (the :chords loop) holds up a sign showing the harmony [I, vi, IV, V] for four measures at a time, while the drummer (:drums loop) keeps a repeating two-measure beat, and the soloist (:melody loop) uses the conductor’s current sign to guide their improvisational notes, ensuring they are always moving harmoniously towards the next section.
You can't put source citations in the Sonic Pi code, or it'll break the code.

That is a crucial observation regarding Sonic Pi syntax, and you are absolutely correct: you cannot put source citations directly in the executable code, or it will break the code
.
Sonic Pi interprets text based on its role, expecting specific commands (play, sleep, sample) or accepted parameters (options/opts)
. Placing arbitrary text or symbols into the script would result in a syntax or runtime error, causing the code to fail or crash
.
To safely include explanatory text, reminders, or references (like source citations) within a Sonic Pi script, you must use a comment
:
In Sonic Pi, a comment is created by adding a hash symbol (#) at the beginning of the line
.
When the code runs, this symbol tells the computer to ignore that specific line
.
Programmers use comments not only to document what their code is doing but also to easily remove or add things into the mix without deleting the actual code
.
For example, if you wanted to note a reference or rule next to a musical line, you would write it like this:
play 60 # This is a comment, and the code runs correctly.
[9] play 65 # This is an error because [9] is not a valid command.
In the first example, the compiler ignores everything after the #, allowing the music to play. In the second example, the code breaks because it does not recognize 
 as valid Sonic Pi language
.

Sonic Pi uses the hash symbol (#) to indicate that the rest of the line should be ignored, treating it as a comment
. Comments are essential for documenting code but must never be mistaken for executable statements
.

Fixed Sonic Pi Popular Song Skeleton
This script uses the recommended live_loop structure for concurrency
 and utilizes the Time State system (set and get) for reliable synchronization between musical elements
.
# --- GLOBAL SETUP ---
use_bpm 120 # Set tempo to 120 BPM [8, 9]
use_debug false # Recommended for performance stability [10]

# --- HARMONY: I-vi-IV-V in C Major using supported chord names ---
# The chord function automatically returns a ring of notes [11, 12].
# Using :major7, :minor7, and :dom7 as required by Sonic Pi 4.5.1 syntax [13, 14].
chords = (ring 
  chord(:C3, :major7), # I - C Major 7th
  chord(:A3, :minor7), # vi - A Minor 7th
  chord(:F3, :major7), # IV - F Major 7th
  chord(:G3, :dom7)    # V - G Dominant 7th
)

# Initialize global Time State for sharing the current chord [6, 7].
set :current_chord, chords.first


# --- 1. HARMONY LOOP (4 bars per chord = 16 beats) ---
# This loop establishes the harmonic context.
live_loop :harmony_change do
  # Step (tick) through the chords ring every cycle [15].
  current_c = chords.tick
  
  # Store the current chord in the Time State for other loops to read [6].
  set :current_chord, current_c
  
  # Play the background chord (long release for sustained presence)
  use_synth :dsaw # Using a moderate synth to avoid CPU overload [16]
  play current_c, release: 16, amp: 0.2, pan: 0 # Sustain for the full 4 bars
  
  sleep 16 # Wait 4 bars (16 beats)
end


# --- 2. RHYTHM LOOP (2-bar repeat with variation) ---
live_loop :drums do
  # Rhythmic Repeat Cycle: 8 beats (2 bars)
  
  # Bar 1: Kick on 1, Snare on 2, Kick on 3, Snare on 4 [17]
  sample :drum_heavy_kick, amp: 1.2 # Kick on beat 1
  sleep 1
  sample :drum_snare_hard, amp: 0.8 # Snare on beat 2
  sleep 1
  sample :drum_heavy_kick, amp: 1.1 # Kick on beat 3
  sleep 0.5
  sample :drum_cymbal_closed, pan: rrand(-0.2, 0.2), amp: 0.4 # Hi-hat variation [18]
  sleep 0.5
  
  # Bar 2: Rhythmic Variation
  sample :drum_snare_hard, amp: 0.8 # Snare on beat 4
  sleep 1
  
  # Hi-hat layer (using 16th notes for groove)
  4.times do # Play 4/16th notes
    sample :drum_cymbal_closed, pan: rrand(-0.7, 0.7), amp: 0.3 # Random pan for texture [19]
    sleep 0.25
  end
  
  # The total sleep time within the loop body must equal the desired loop duration (8 beats)
end


# --- 3. MELODY LOOP (8-bar phrase with smart anchoring) ---
live_loop :melody do
  # Sync to the harmony loop start to align the 8-bar phrase cycle (16 beats * 2) [20].
  sync :harmony_change 
  
  use_synth :piano # Using a traditional instrument voice [21]
  use_synth_defaults attack: 0.05, release: 0.25, amp: 1.0 # Set short A/R for clarity [19]
  
  base_scale = (scale :C5, :major, num_octaves: 1).ring # Define the working scale [22].

  # Melody Phrase (32 steps = 8 bars @ 4 beats/bar)
  32.times do |i|
    # Check the current chord set by the :harmony_change loop [23].
    current_c = get[:current_chord] 
    
    # Smart Anchoring: Choose either a chord tone (anchor) or a scale tone (motion)
    n = if one_in(4) # 1 in 4 chance to choose a strong chord tone (consonance/anchor) [24, 25]
          current_c.choose 
        else
          base_scale.choose # High movement/tension (emotional motion) [26, 27]
        end

    play n
    
    # Introduce small rhythmic variation (e.g., occasional 8th note, otherwise 16th note)
    if one_in(8)
      sleep 0.5 
    else
      sleep 0.25 
    end
  end
end
Explanation of Sonic Pi Features and Musical Components
Programming Structure:
The code is built around the live_loop construct, which enables concurrent playback and modification of the music while the code is running (Live Coding)
. Each musical component (Harmony, Rhythm, Melody) runs in its own independent thread
.
Harmony (:harmony_change):
Progression: A common Western music progression (I-vi-IV-V) is implemented using 7th chords for a richer sound (Cmaj7, Am7, Fmaj7, Gdom7)
.
Time State (set): The progression loop is the conductor. Every 16 beats (4 bars), it uses .tick to advance to the next chord in the chords ring
 and uses set :current_chord, current_c to store that chord list globally
.
Duration: The play current_c, release: 16 command ensures the synth note sustains (holds amplitude) for 16 beats, or 4 measures, creating a slow harmonic rhythm
.
Rhythm (:drums):
Instrumentation: Traditional samples like :drum_heavy_kick and :drum_snare_hard are used to establish a fundamental backbeat
.
Repeat/Variation: The loop is structured to repeat every 8 beats (2 bars)
. Variation is achieved through slight modifications in the timing of the hi-hat and the use of the rrand(-0.7, 0.7) parameter with pan: on the hi-hat, adding stereo movement and texture
.
Melody (:melody):
Synchronization (sync): The melody loop uses sync :harmony_change
. This ensures the start of the 8-bar melody phrase aligns perfectly with the start of the 4-bar chord progression cycle, preventing accidental timing offsets
.
Melody-Chord Anchoring: This fulfills the "smart anchoring" requirement by accessing the current harmonic context using current_c = get[:current_chord]
.
The logic uses probabilistic control via one_in(4)
 to randomly select whether the note n should be chosen from the strictly consonant current_c (the current chord) or from the broader base_scale (C Major scale)
.
This blend of anchoring (consonance) and motion (using non-chord scale tones) helps to create the desired emotional motion in the melody line.
This structure is robust and optimized for performance by separating the time-critical harmonic updating (via Time State) from the heavy sonic generation, a practice recommended for stability, especially when dealing with complex arrangements
.

Sonic Pi Popular Song Skeleton (Syntactically Fixed)
# --- GLOBAL SETUP ---
use_bpm 120 # Set tempo to 120 BPM
use_debug false # Recommended for performance stability and reducing log output [1, 4]

# --- HARMONY: I-vi-IV-V in C Major ---
# Renamed variable to 'chord_sequence' to avoid any conflict with the built-in 'chord' function.
# We use standard, supported 7th chord qualities for Sonic Pi v4.5.1 [5].
chord_sequence = (ring 
  chord(:C3, :major7), # I - C Major 7th
  chord(:A3, :minor7), # vi - A Minor 7th
  chord(:F3, :major7), # IV - F Major 7th
  chord(:G3, :dom7)    # V - G Dominant 7th (Standard 7th)
)

# Initialize global Time State to share the current chord across threads [6, 7].
set :current_chord, chord_sequence.first


# --- 1. HARMONY LOOP (4 bars per chord = 16 beats) ---
live_loop :harmony_change do
  # Step (tick) through the chord_sequence ring every cycle [8].
  current_c = chord_sequence.tick
  
  # Store the current chord list in the Time State [6].
  set :current_chord, current_c
  
  # Play the sustained chord using the :dsaw synth, which is considered lightweight/safe [9, 10].
  use_synth :dsaw 
  # Release set to 16 beats (4 bars) for sustained presence [11].
  play current_c, release: 16, amp: 0.2, pan: 0 
  
  sleep 16 # Wait 4 bars (16 beats)
end


# --- 2. RHYTHM LOOP (2-bar repeat with variation) ---
live_loop :drums do
  # Rhythmic Repeat Cycle: 8 beats (2 bars)
  
  # Bar 1 (4 beats) - Basic Kick/Snare Pattern
  sample :drum_heavy_kick, amp: 1.2 # Use samples for traditional instrumentation [12, 13].
  sleep 1
  sample :drum_snare_hard, amp: 0.8
  sleep 1
  sample :drum_heavy_kick, amp: 1.1 
  sleep 1
  sample :drum_snare_hard, amp: 0.8
  sleep 1
  
  # Bar 2 (4 beats) - Hi-Hat Variation
  # Introduce off-beat hi-hats (16th notes feel) for variation [14].
  8.times do # 8 steps cover 2 beats (8 * 0.25 = 2.0)
    # Use pan: rrand for stereo texture [15, 16].
    sample :drum_cymbal_closed, pan: rrand(-0.5, 0.5), amp: 0.3 
    sleep 0.25 
  end
  
  # Kick/Snare on remaining 2 beats
  sample :drum_heavy_kick, amp: 1.2
  sleep 1
  sample :drum_snare_hard, amp: 0.8
  sleep 1
  
  # Total sleep is 8 beats (2 bars).
end


# --- 3. MELODY LOOP (8-Bar Phrase with Anchoring) ---
live_loop :melody do
  # Sync to the harmony loop start to align the 8-bar phrase cycle (32 beats) [17].
  sync :harmony_change
  
  use_synth :pluck # Using a traditional instrument voice (lightweight synth recommended for stability [10]).
  use_synth_defaults attack: 0.05, release: 0.25, amp: 1.0 
  
  base_scale = (scale :C5, :major, num_octaves: 1).ring # Define the working scale ring [18, 19].

  # Melody Phrase: 8 bars * 4 beats/bar = 32 steps.
  32.times do 
    current_c = get[:current_chord] # Anchoring: retrieve the current chord from Time State [20].
    
    # Smart Anchoring: 1 in 4 chance to choose a strong chord tone (anchor), otherwise use a scale tone (motion) [21].
    n = if one_in(4) 
          current_c.choose # Choose a note from the current chord (consonance) [22].
        else
          base_scale.choose # Choose a note from the broader scale (emotional motion) [22].
        end

    play n
    
    # Using stable sleep value for consistent timing.
    sleep 0.25 
  end
end
Key Sonic Pi Safety Measures Applied
The script now follows critical best practices for stable execution:
Reserved Name Avoidance: The progression array is definitively named chord_sequence to avoid the conflict with the built-in chord function [i, 114].
Concurrency Management: The use of live_loop enables concurrent processes (rhythm, harmony, melody)
.
Thread-Safe State: Shared information (the current chord) is managed exclusively through the Time State system (set :current_chord / get[:current_chord])
. This is essential for deterministic and reliable sharing of information across concurrent loops, preventing "race conditions" which cause non-deterministic behavior when using regular variables
.
Synchronization: The melody loop uses sync :harmony_change to ensure its 8-bar phrase starts precisely when the chord loop starts its cycle, overcoming any potential accidental timing offsets
.
Performance: The script favors light synthesizers (:dsaw, :pluck) and moderate complexity, a practical workaround recommended for stability on performance-constrained environments like Windows 4.5.1 to prevent CPU overload and timing drift
.

These progressive arrays can be integrated into the skeleton structure we developed previously, using the .tick function within the main chord loop to step through the sequence
. By changing the array name in the :harmony_change loop, you instantly switch the harmonic backbone of your song, maintaining the same rhythm and melodic structure dynamics.
How do I do this properly?


chords_nice = [
chord(:B4, :major7),
chord(:D4, :major7),
chord(:E4, :minor7),
chord(:C4, :major7)
]


chords_alternate = [
chord(:C4, :major7),
chord(:G4, :major7),
chord(:A4, :minor7),
chord(:F4, :major7)
]


chords = [choose(chords_nice, chords_alternate)].choose

This is an insightful query regarding how to manage complex musical structures and introduce randomization in Sonic Pi. Your goal is to randomly select an entire chord progression array before it is played.
The issue in your proposed syntax is the redundant and incorrect use of square brackets and choose: chords = [choose(chords_nice, chords_alternate)].choose. The choose function should take a single list (or array) of choices as its argument
.
To implement this properly and reliably within Sonic Pi, especially when running multiple concurrent loops (as in our skeleton script), we need to ensure we are utilizing rings for looping and the choose function correctly to select between collections of notes
.
Here is how to properly structure your code to choose between the two progressions, followed by several methods for implementing this harmonic control.
Proper Definition and Random Selection Syntax
First, the two chord progressions should be explicitly defined as rings
. Rings are preferred because they automatically wrap around when iterating through elements, which is essential for live_loop and the .tick function
.
# 1. Define chord progressions as Rings of Chord Lists
chords_nice = (ring
  chord(:B4, :major7),
  chord(:D4, :major7),
  chord(:E4, :minor7),
  chord(:C4, :major7)
) # All chord qualities used here are supported [8]

chords_alternate = (ring
  chord(:C4, :major7),
  chord(:G4, :major7),
  chord(:A4, :minor7),
  chord(:F4, :major7)
)

# 2. Define a list containing the two progression rings
# The items in this list are the two rings defined above.
progression_options = [chords_nice, chords_alternate]

# 3. Select ONE progression (one ring) randomly from the list when the script starts
# This uses the choose function to pick one item from the array [2].
selected_progression = progression_options.choose
Once selected_progression is set (e.g., it now holds the chords_nice ring), you use this variable inside your live_loop with .tick to iterate through the sequence.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Different Methods for Harmonic Control
How you integrate this selection depends on whether you want the progression choice to be permanent per run, or dynamic during the performance.
Method 1: Fixed Random Progression (Easiest and Most Deterministic)
This method chooses one progression once when you hit Run, and that progression plays forever
. This relies on pseudo-randomness, ensuring that if you use use_random_seed elsewhere, the selection remains repeatable
.
# --- SETUP (Outside Live Loop) ---
# 1. Define options as rings (ensuring cycles are possible)
chords_nice = (ring chord(:B4, :major7), chord(:D4, :major7), chord(:E4, :minor7), chord(:C4, :major7))
chords_alternate = (ring chord(:C4, :major7), chord(:G4, :major7), chord(:A4, :minor7), chord(:F4, :major7))

# 2. Select one progression globally
# The variable 'fixed_progression' now holds one of the two rings.
fixed_progression = [chords_nice, chords_alternate].choose


# --- HARMONY LOOP ---
live_loop :harmony_change do
  # 3. Use .tick on the chosen progression to advance
  current_harmony = fixed_progression.tick
  
  # Store the active chord for melody/bass loops to read (Time State is thread-safe [11])
  set :active_harmony_list, current_harmony
  
  use_synth :dsaw
  play current_harmony, release: 16, amp: 0.2
  
  sleep 16 # 4 bars per chord
end
Method 2: Dynamic Random Progression (Switching on the Fly)
If you want the harmonic cycle to randomly switch between the chords_nice progression and the chords_alternate progression periodically (e.g., every 64 beats), you must nest the choices. This introduces dynamic randomization using the choose function inside the loop
.
# --- SETUP (Outside Live Loop) ---
chords_nice = (ring chord(:B4, :major7), chord(:D4, :major7), chord(:E4, :minor7), chord(:C4, :major7))
chords_alternate = (ring chord(:C4, :major7), chord(:G4, :major7), chord(:A4, :minor7), chord(:F4, :major7))

# Create a ring where elements alternate between the two full progression rings
# This is a ring of rings: it contains [Ring A, Ring B, Ring A, Ring B...]
prog_cycle = (ring chords_nice, chords_alternate).tick(:prog_choice)


# --- HARMONY LOOP (Switches progression array every 4 bars/16 beats) ---
live_loop :harmony_change do
  # 1. Select which Progression Ring to use based on the tick value
  # We use .look to retrieve the current progression ring without advancing the tick [12].
  current_progression_ring = (ring chords_nice, chords_alternate).look(:prog_choice)
  
  # 2. Pick the NEXT chord from the current Progression Ring (A or B)
  current_harmony = current_progression_ring.tick
  set :active_harmony_list, current_harmony
  
  use_synth :dsaw
  play current_harmony, release: 4, amp: 0.2
  
  # Check if we have completed a full chord cycle (4 chords * 4 beats/chord = 16 beats)
  # When the internal tick count reaches 4 (i.e., we played 4 chords), advance the progression choice.
  if tick_get(:prog_choice) % 4 == 0
    (ring chords_nice, chords_alternate).tick(:prog_choice) # Advance the choice every 4 chord ticks
  end
  
  sleep 4 # 1 bar per chord (speeding up harmonic rhythm for demonstration)
end
Why Use ring?
In Sonic Pi, the .tick method is designed to be used effectively with rings
. A standard Ruby array ([...]) would eventually run out of elements, causing the code to stop or return nil
. A ring structure handles indices that go beyond its size by wrapping back to the beginning, ensuring the music never stops in a loop
. Since both chord and scale functions automatically return rings, or can be easily converted with .ring, they are the best structures for sequences within live_loops
.
As demonstrated in Method 1, the variable holding the chosen progression (e.g., fixed_progression) stores the array of notes, and placing the random selection outside the live_loop ensures that the choice is stable until the next time the entire script is run
.

Explanation of Syntax
By using the structure variable = [(ring chord1, chord2, ...)], you are defining a container [] that holds a single, looping sequence (ring) of chords.
The chord function itself creates a list of notes for a specified root and quality
. Wrapping these lists in a ring makes the sequence cycle indefinitely, which is ideal for integration into a live_loop using .tick
. You can then randomly select one of these chords_alt variables using .choose at the start of your script if desired
.
